However, the Crown enjoys [inviolability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inviolability) in the performance of these [prerogatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_prerogative#Spain) and [cannot be prosecuted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_immunity) in the very courts which administer justice in its name.[^cite_note-148] For this reason, every official act done by the monarch requires the [countersignature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countersignature) of the [prime minister](203556.md) or, when appropriate, the [president of the Congress of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Congress_of_Deputies) to have the force of law. The countersigning procedure or *refrendo* in turn transfers political and legal liability for the royal prerogative to the attesting parties.[^cite_note-149] This provision does not apply to the [Royal Household](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Household_of_Spain), over which the monarch enjoys absolute control and supervision, or to membership in the [Order of the Golden Fleece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Golden_Fleece), which is a [dynastic order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynastic_order) in the personal gift of the [House of Bourbon-Anjou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_royal_family).[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

The royal prerogatives may be classified by whether they are ministerial functions or reserve powers. Ministerial functions are those royal prerogatives that are, pursuant to the [convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_convention_\(political_custom\)) established by [Juan Carlos I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Carlos_I), performed by the monarch after soliciting the [advice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advice_\(constitutional_law\)) of the Government, the Congress of Deputies, the Senate, the General Council of the Judiciary, or the Constitutional Tribunal, as the case may be. On the other hand, the [reserve powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_powers) of the Crown are those royal prerogatives which are exercised in the monarch's personal discretion.[^cite_note-Walther_L._Bernecker_65–84-144] Most of the Crown's royal prerogatives are ministerial in practice, meaning the monarch has no discretion in their execution and primarily performs them as a matter of state ceremonial. Nevertheless, when performing said ministerial functions, the monarch has the right to be consulted before acting on advice, the right to encourage a particular course of policy or action, and the right to warn the responsible constitutional authorities against the same. Those ministerial functions are as follows:&#x20;

1. [Sanction](153067.md) and [promulgate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promulgation) bills duly passed by the Cortes Generales, making them [laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Spain). The Spanish Constitution mandates the monarch grant royal assent to each bill within fifteen days of its passage. The monarch does not have a right to [veto](74661.md) legislation.

   [^cite_note-151]

   [^cite_note-152]

2. [Summon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writ_of_summons) the Cortes Generales into [session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_session) following a general election, [dissolve](652293.md) the same upon the expiration of its four-year term, and [proclaim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation) the [election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Spain) of the next Cortes. These functions are performed in accordance with the strictures of the Spanish Constitution.

   [^cite_note-153]

   [^cite_note-154]

   [^cite_note-155]

   [^cite_note-156]

   [^cite_note-157]

3. Appoint and dismiss [ministers of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_government_departments) on the advice of the prime minister.

   [^cite_note-158]

4. Appoint the president of the [Supreme Court](8327853.md) on the advice of the General Council of the Judiciary.

   [^cite_note-159]

5. Appoint the president of the [Constitutional Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Spain) from among its members, on the advice of the full bench, for a term of three years.

   [^cite_note-160]

6. Appoint the [Fiscal General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Attorney_General), who leads the [Prosecution Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecution_Ministry), on the advice of the Government. Before tendering advice, the Government is required to consult the General Council of the Judiciary.

   [^cite_note-161]

7. Appoint the [presidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_\(Autonomous_Community_of_Spain\)) of the [autonomous communities](55002.md) as elected by their respective parliaments.

   [^cite_note-162]

8. Issue [decrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decrees) approved in the Council of Ministers, confer [civil service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_State_Administration) and [military appointments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioned_officer), and award [honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orders,_decorations,_and_medals_of_Spain) and [distinctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_nobility) in the gift of the state. These functions are performed on the advice of the prime minister or another minister designated thereby.

   [^cite_note-163]

   [^cite_note-164]

9. Exercise [supreme command and control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) over the [Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Armed_Forces), on the advice of the prime minister.

   [^cite_note-165]

10. [Declare war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_war) and [make peace](404505.md) on the advice of the prime minister and with the prior authorization of the Cortes Generales.

    [^cite_note-166]

11. [Ratify](429650.md) [treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaties), on the advice of the prime minister.

    [^cite_note-167]

12. Accredit Spanish [ambassadors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambassadors) and [ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Envoy_\(title\)) to foreign states and [receive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrement) the [credentials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_of_credence) of [foreign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Spain) [diplomats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_rank) to Spain, on the advice of the prime minister.

    [^cite_note-168]

13. Exercise the [right of clemency](288593.md), but without the authority to grant [general pardons](18940624.md), on the advice of the prime minister.

    [^cite_note-169]

14. Patronise the Royal Academies.

    [^cite_note-170]

    [^cite_note-171]

The aforesaid limitations do not apply to the exercise of the Crown's reserve powers, which may be invoked by the monarch when [necessary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctrine_of_necessity) to maintain the continuity and stability of state institutions.[^cite_note-172] For example, the monarch has the right to be kept informed on affairs of state through regular [audiences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audience_\(meeting\)) with the Government. For this purpose, the monarch may preside at any time over meetings of the Council of Ministers, but only when requested by the prime minister.[^cite_note-173] The monarch may prematurely dissolve the Congress of Deputies, the Senate, or both houses of the Cortes in their entirety before the expiration of their four-year term and, in consequence thereof, concurrently call for [snap elections](462915.md). The monarch exercises this prerogative on the request of the prime minister, after the matter has been discussed by the Council of Ministers. The monarch may choose to accept or refuse the request.[^cite_note-174] The monarch may also order national referendums on the request of the prime minister, but only with the prior authorisation of the Cortes Generales. Again, the monarch may choose to accept or refuse the prime minister's request.[^cite_note-175]&#x20;

The Crown's reserve powers further extend into [constitutional interpretation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_interpretation) and the [administration of justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administration_of_justice). The monarch appoints the twenty members of the [General Council of the Judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Council_of_the_Judiciary). Of these counselors, twelve are nominated by the supreme, appellate and trial courts, four are nominated by the Congress of Deputies by a majority of three-fifths of its members, and four are nominated by the Senate with the same majority. The monarch may choose to accept or refuse any nomination.[^cite_note-176] In a similar vein, the monarch appoints the twelve magistrates of the [Constitutional Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Spain). Of these magistrates, four magistrates are nominated by the Congress of Deputies by a majority of three-fifths of its members, four magistrates are nominated by the Senate with the same majority, two magistrates are nominated by the Government, and two magistrates are nominated by the General Council of the Judiciary. The monarch may choose to accept or refuse any nomination.[^cite_note-177]&#x20;

It is the monarch's reserve powers concerning [Government formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_formation) that are perhaps the most frequently exercised. The monarch nominates a candidate for [prime minister](203556.md) and, as the case may be, appoints or removes them from office based on the prime minister's ability to maintain the [confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motions_of_no_confidence_in_Spain) of the [Congress of Deputies](352254.md).[^cite_note-178] If the Congress of Deputies fails to give its confidence to a new Government within two months, and is incapable of governing as a result of parliamentary gridlock, the monarch may dissolve the Cortes Generales and call for fresh elections. The monarch makes use of these reserve powers in their own deliberative judgment after consulting the president of the Congress of Deputies.[^cite_note-Section_99-179]&#x20;

#### Cortes Generales



The hemicycle of the [Congress of Deputies](352254.md)

[Legislative authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_branch) vests in the *[Cortes Generales](519703.md)* (English: Spanish Parliament, lit. 'General Courts'), a democratically elected [bicameral parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameral_parliament) that serves as the supreme representative body of the Spanish people. Aside from the Crown, it is the only basic State institution that enjoys inviolability.[^cite_note-180] It comprises the [Congress of Deputies](352254.md) (*Congreso de los Diputados*), a [lower house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_house) with 350 deputies, and the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Senate) (*Senado*), an [upper house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_house) with 266 senators.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182]&#x20;

Deputies are elected by [popular vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election) on [closed lists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closed_list) via [proportional representation](23298.md) to serve four-year terms.[^cite_note-183] 208 senators are directly elected by popular vote using a [limited voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited_voting) method, with the remaining 58 senators appointed by the [regional legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Spanish_regional_legislatures) to also serve four-year terms.[^cite_note-184]&#x20;

#### Government

[Executive authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_branch) rests with the [Government](1306113.md) (*Gobierno de España*), which is [collectively responsible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_collective_responsibility) to the Congress of Deputies.[^cite_note-Section_97-185][^cite_note-186] It consists of the [prime minister](203556.md), one or more [deputy prime ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Spain), and the [ministers of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_government_departments).[^cite_note-187] These characters together constitute the [Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_of_Spain) which, as Spain's [central executive authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_government), conducts the business of the Government and administers the [civil service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_State_Administration).[^cite_note-Section_97-185] The Government remains in office so long as it can maintain the [confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motions_of_no_confidence_in_Spain) of the Congress of Deputies.&#x20;

The prime minister, as [head of government](37570.md), has primacy over the other ministers by virtue of their ability to [advise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advice_\(constitutional_law\)) the monarch as to their appointment and dismissal.[^cite_note-188] The prime minister has plenary authority conferred by the Spanish Constitution to direct and coordinate the Government's policies and administrative actions.[^cite_note-189] The Spanish monarch [nominates](203556.md#Royal_nomination_and_congressional_confirmation) the prime minister after consulting representatives from the different parliamentary groups and appoints them to office upon a vote of investiture in the Congress of Deputies.[^cite_note-Section_99-179]&#x20;

Some political [corruption in Spain](40267700.md) has been found.[^cite_note-e377-190]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [History of the territorial organization of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_territorial_organization_of_Spain) and [Political divisions of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_divisions_of_Spain)

#### Autonomous communities

Main article: [Autonomous communities of Spain](55002.md)

See also: [Nationalities and regions of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalities_and_regions_of_Spain)



Autonomous communities of Spain

Spain's autonomous communities are the first level administrative divisions of the country. They were created after the 1978 constitution came into effect in recognition of the right to self-government of the "[*nationalities* and regions of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalities_and_regions_of_Spain)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpanish_Constitution1978Article_143-191] The autonomous communities were to comprise adjacent provinces with common historical, cultural, and economic traits. This territorial organisation, based on [devolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution), is known in Spain as the "State of Autonomies" (*Estado de las Autonomías*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpanish_Constitution1978Article_147-192]&#x20;

The basic institutional law of each autonomous community is the [Statute of Autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy). The Statutes of Autonomy establish the name of the community according to its historical and contemporary identity, the limits of its territories, the name and organisation of the institutions of government and the rights they enjoy according to the constitution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpanish_Constitution1978Article_147-192] This ongoing process of devolution means that, while officially a [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state), Spain is nevertheless one of the most [decentralised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decentralised) countries in Europe, along with [federations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation) like [Belgium](3343.md), [Germany](11867.md), and [Switzerland](26748.md).[^cite_note-193]&#x20;

Catalonia, Galicia and the Basque Country, which identified themselves as *nationalities*, were granted self-government through a rapid process. Andalusia also identified itself as a nationality in its first Statute of Autonomy, even though it followed the longer process stipulated in the constitution for the rest of the country. Progressively, other communities in revisions to their Statutes of Autonomy have also taken that denomination in accordance with their historical and modern identities, such as the Valencian Community,[^cite_note-194] the Canary Islands,[^cite_note-195] the Balearic Islands,[^cite_note-196] and Aragon.[^cite_note-197]&#x20;

The autonomous communities have wide legislative and executive autonomy, with their own elected parliaments and governments as well as their own dedicated [public administrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Administration_of_Spain). The distribution of powers may be different for every community, as laid out in their Statutes of Autonomy, since devolution was intended to be asymmetrical. For instance, only two communities—the Basque Country and Navarre—have full fiscal autonomy based on ancient [foral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuero) provisions. Each autonomous community is responsible for healthcare and education, among other public services.[^cite_note-198]&#x20;

Beyond these competencies, the *nationalities*—[Andalusia](2736.md), the [Basque Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pais_Vasco), [Catalonia](6822.md), and [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Spain\))—were also devolved more powers than the other communities, among them the ability of the regional president to dissolve the parliament and call for elections at any time. In addition, the Basque Country, the [Canary Islands](5717.md), Catalonia, and [Navarre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navarre) each have autonomous police corps of their own: *[Ertzaintza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ertzaintza)*, *[Policía Canaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polic%C3%ADa_Canaria)*, *[Mossos d'Esquadra](294120.md)*, and *[Policía Foral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polic%C3%ADa_Foral)* respectively. Other communities have shared units with the [National Police Corps](1608789.md) or no regional police forces at all.[^cite_note-199]&#x20;

#### Provinces and municipalities

Main articles: [Local government in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Spain), [Provinces of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Spain), and [Municipalities of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Spain)

Autonomous communities are divided into [provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Spain), which serve as their territorial building blocks. Provinces are divided into [municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Spain). The existence of both the provinces and the municipalities is guaranteed and protected by the constitution, not necessarily by the Statutes of Autonomy themselves. Municipalities are granted autonomy to manage their internal affairs. Provinces are the territorial divisions designed to carry out the activities of the State.[^cite_note-200]&#x20;

The current provincial division structure is based—with minor changes—on the [1833 territorial division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1833_territorial_division_of_Spain) by [Javier de Burgos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javier_de_Burgos).[^cite_note-201] The Spanish territory is divided into 50 provinces. The communities of Asturias, Cantabria, La Rioja, the Balearic Islands, Madrid, Murcia and Navarre are the only communities that comprise a single province, which is coextensive with the community itself. In these cases, the administrative institutions of the province are replaced by the governmental institutions of the community.[^cite_note-202]&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Spain)



The [Royal Palace of Pedralbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Pedralbes) in Barcelona, headquarters of the [Union for the Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_for_the_Mediterranean)

After the return of democracy following the death of [Franco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Franco) in 1975, Spain's [foreign policy](233404.md) priorities were to break out of the diplomatic isolation of the [Franco years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_under_Franco) and expand [diplomatic relations](28165911.md), enter the [European Community](9317.md), and define security relations with the West.&#x20;

As a member of [NATO](21133.md) since 1982, Spain has established itself as a participant in multilateral international security activities. Spain's EU membership represents an important part of its foreign policy. On a wide range of issues, Spain often prefers to coordinate its efforts with its EU partners through the European political cooperation mechanisms. In addition to being represented via EU membership, Spain is a permanently invited guest to all [G20](2206608.md) summits.[^cite_note-203]&#x20;

Spain has maintained its special relations with [Hispanic America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_America) and the [Philippines](23440.md). Its policy emphasises the concept of an [Ibero-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibero-America) community, essentially the renewal of the concept of *"[Hispanidad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanidad)"* or *"[Hispanismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanism)"*, as it is often referred to in English, which has sought to link the Iberian Peninsula with Hispanic America through language, commerce, history and culture. It is fundamentally "based on shared values and the recovery of democracy."[^cite_note-204]&#x20;

Spain is involved in a number of [territorial disputes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Spain#Disputes). [Spain claims Gibraltar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disputed_status_of_Gibraltar), an [Overseas Territory of the United Kingdom](207114.md), in the southernmost part of the Iberian Peninsula.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] Another dispute surrounds the [Savage Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savage_Islands); Spain claims that they are rocks rather than islands, and therefore does not accept the Portuguese [Exclusive Economic Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_Economic_Zone) (200 nautical miles) generated by the islands.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] Spain claims sovereignty over the [Perejil Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perejil_Island), a small, uninhabited rocky [islet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islet) located in the South shore of the [Strait of Gibraltar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Gibraltar). It was the subject of an armed incident between Spain and Morocco in 2002. Morocco claims the Spanish cities of [Ceuta](6443.md) and [Melilla](20226.md) and the *[plazas de soberanía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plazas_de_soberan%C3%ADa)* islets off the northern coast of Africa. Portugal does not recognise Spain's sovereignty over the territory of [Olivenza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivenza).[^cite_note-210]&#x20;

### Military

Main article: [Spanish Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Armed_Forces)



[Amphibious assault ship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibious_assault_ship)-[aircraft carrier](2219.md) [*Juan Carlos I*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_ship_Juan_Carlos_I)

The [Spanish Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Armed_Forces) are divided into three branches: [Army (*Ejército de Tierra*)](2142325.md); [Navy (*Armada*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Navy); and [Air and Space Force (*Ejército del Aire y del Espacio*)](3853398.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpanish_Constitution1978Article_8-211] The armed forces of Spain are known as the Spanish Armed Forces (*Fuerzas Armadas Españolas*). Their [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) is the King of Spain, [Felipe VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_VI_of_Spain).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpanish_Constitution1978Article_62-212] The next military authorities in line are the Prime Minister and the Minister of Defence. The fourth military authority of the State is the [Chief of the Defence Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_the_Defence_Staff_\(Spain\)) (JEMAD).[^cite_note-213] The [Defence Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_Staff_\(Spain\)) (*Estado Mayor de la Defensa*) assists the JEMAD as auxiliary body.&#x20;

The Spanish armed forces are a professional force with a strength in 2017 of 121,900 active personnel and 4,770 reserve personnel. Spain also has the 77,000 strong [Civil Guard](325008.md) which comes under the control of the Ministry of defence in times of a national emergency. In 2015, the Spanish defence budget was 5.71 billion euros (US\$7.2 billion), with a 1% increase. The increase comes because of security concerns.[^cite_note-214] Military conscription ended in 2001.[^cite_note-215]&#x20;

According to the 2024 [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index), Spain is the 23rd most peaceful country in the world.[^cite_note-216]&#x20;

### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Spain)

See also: [LGBT rights in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Spain)

The [Spanish Constitution of 1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Constitution_of_1978) "protect all Spaniards and all the peoples of Spain in the exercise of human rights, their cultures and traditions, languages and institutions".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpanish_Constitution1978preamble-217] According to [Amnesty International](18947898.md) (AI), government investigations of alleged police abuses are often lengthy and punishments were light.[^cite_note-218] Violence against women was a problem, which the Government took steps to address.[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] Spain provides one of the highest degrees of liberty in the world for its [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT) community. Among the countries studied by [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) in 2013, Spain is rated first in acceptance of homosexuality, with 88% of those surveyed saying that homosexuality should be accepted.[^cite_note-221]&#x20;

The Cortes Generales approved the *Gender Equality Act* in 2007 aimed at furthering [equality between genders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_equality) in Spanish political and economic life.[^cite_note-222] According to [Inter-Parliamentary Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Parliamentary_Union) data for September 2018, 137 of the 350 members of the Congress were women (39.1%), while in the Senate, there were 101 women out of 266 (39.9%), placing Spain 16th on their list of countries ranked by proportion of women in the [lower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_House) (or [single](204304.md)) House.[^cite_note-223] In 2008, the [Gender Empowerment Measure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_Empowerment_Measure) of Spain in the United Nations [Human Development Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Report) was 0.794, 12th in the world.[^cite_note-224]&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Spain](27393.md)



The [Cuatro Torres Business Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuatro_Torres_Business_Area) in [Madrid](41188263.md)



[Torre Glòries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_Gl%C3%B2ries) and the [22@](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/22@) business district in [Barcelona](4443.md)

Spain has a [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) that [combines elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_market_economy) of [free-market capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free-market_capitalist) with [social welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_spending) and [state intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_intervention). It is one of [21 countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trillion_dollar_club_\(macroeconomics\)) with a [nominal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominal_GDP) [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP) exceeding \$1 [trillion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trillion_\(short_scale\)) per year, ranking [12th largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) worldwide and [fourth largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_in_Europe_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) both in the [European Union](9317.md) and within the [eurozone](184391.md). Spain is classified as a [high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank_high-income_economy) by the [World Bank](36752.md) and an [advanced economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_economy) by the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md). As of 2024, it is the fastest growing major advanced economy in the world,[^cite_note-225] growing nearly four times higher than the eurozone average.[^cite_note-economist.com-226]&#x20;

Spain began [industrialising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialisation) in the late 18th century, albeit more gradually and unevenly than other European countries; industry was limited mostly to Catalonia (primarily textile manufacturing) and the [Basque Country](20514897.md) (iron and steel production).\[^cite_note-[www.britannica.com-2024-227](http://www.britannica.com-2024-227/)] Overall economic growth was slower than in most major western European countries, and Spain remained relatively underdeveloped by the early 20th century.\[^cite_note-[www.britannica.com-2024-227](http://www.britannica.com-2024-227/)]&#x20;

The [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War), followed by failed [autarkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autarky) and interventionist policies that were worsened by [international isolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_isolation), left the economy on the brink of collapse by the late 1950s. [Technocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technocracy) reforms were enacted to avert the crisis, laying the groundwork for the [Spanish economic miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_miracle), a period of rapid growth from 1960 until 1974, during which Spain's economy grew an average of 6.6 percent per year, exceeding every country except Japan.\[^cite_note-[www.britannica.com-2024-227](http://www.britannica.com-2024-227/)]&#x20;

Since its [transition to democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_transition_to_democracy) in the late 1970s, Spain has generally sought to [liberalise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization) its economy and deepen regional and international [integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_integration). It joined the [European Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Community)—now the European Union—in 1986 and implemented policies and reforms that allowed for its participation in the inaugural launch of the euro in 1999. Spain's largest trade and investment partners are within the EU and eurozone, including its four largest export markets; EU membership also coincided with a tripling of [foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) from 1990 to 2000. Spain was among the countries affected most by the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) and subsequent [European debt crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_debt_crisis), leading to the [2008–2014 Spanish financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008%E2%80%932014_Spanish_financial_crisis).&#x20;

Spain has long struggled with high [unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment_in_Spain), which has never fallen below 8% since the 1980s. In 2013, unemployment hit a peak of 27%.[^cite_note-jopson-2026-228] It stood at 9.93% in January 2026,[^cite_note-unemployment2025-229] the lowest since 2008.[^cite_note-jopson-2026-228] [Youth unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_unemployment_in_Spain) is particularly severe by both global and regional standards; at 24.90% (as of January 2025), it is the highest among EU members and well above the EU average of 14.6%.[^cite_note-unemployment2024-230] Perennial weak points of Spain's economy include a large [informal economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy);[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233] an education system that performs poorly compared to most developed countries;[^cite_note-234] and low rates of private sector investment.[^cite_note-economist.com-226] [Median equivalised disposable income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_income) was \$26,630 per year (2021, PPP).[^cite_note-y148-235]&#x20;

Since the 1990s, which saw a wave of [privatisations](24661.md),[^cite_note-236] several Spanish companies have reached multinational status; they maintain a strong and leading presence in Latin America—where Spain is the second largest foreign investor after the United States—but have also expanded into Asia, especially China and India.[^cite_note-Economist2009bet-237] As of 2023, Spain was home to [eight of the 500 largest companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_Spanish_companies) in the world by annual revenue, according to the *Fortune Global 500;* these include [Banco Santander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banco_Santander), the [14th-largest banking institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_banks) in the world; [electric utility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_utility) [Iberdrola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberdrola), the world's largest renewable energy operator;[^cite_note-238] and [Telefónica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telef%C3%B3nica), [one of the largest telephone operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_telephone_operating_companies) and [mobile network providers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mobile_network_operators). Twenty Spanish companies are listed in the [2023 *Forbes* Global 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_Global_2000) ranking of the 2,000 largest public companies, reflecting diverse sectors such as construction ([ACS Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACS_Group)), aviation ([ENAIRE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ENAIRE)), pharmaceuticals ([Grifols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grifols)), and transportation ([Ferrovial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferrovial)).[^cite_note-Economist2009big-239] Additionally, one of Spain's largest private sector entities is [Mondragon Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mondragon_Corporation), the world's largest [worker-owned cooperative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worker_cooperative).&#x20;

The [automotive industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automotive_industry_in_Spain) is one of the largest employers in the country and a major contributor to economic growth, accounting for one-tenth of [gross domestic product](12594.md) and 18% of total exports (including vehicles and auto-parts). In 2023, Spain produced 2.45 million automobiles—of which over 2.1 million were exported abroad—ranking eighth in the world and second in Europe (after [Germany](11867.md)) by total number.[^cite_note-ICEX-240] In total, 89% of vehicles and 60% of auto-parts manufactured in Spain were exported worldwide in 2023; the total external trade surplus of vehicles alone reached €18.8bn in 2023. Overall, the automotive industry supports nearly two million jobs, or nine percent of the labour force.[^cite_note-ICEX-240]&#x20;

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Spain)



[Benidorm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benidorm), one of Europe's largest coastal tourist destinations

In 2024, Spain was the second most visited country in the world only behind [France](5843419.md), recording 94 million tourists.[^cite_note-spain2024-241][^cite_note-apnews2024-242] The international tourist expenditure in 2024 was around 126 billion euros.[^cite_note-tourists2024-243] The headquarters of the [World Tourism Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_Organization) are located in Madrid. Tourism in Spain is a major contributor to national economic life, with foreign and domestic tourism contributing to 12.3% of Spain's [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP) in 2023.[^cite_note-ine-244]&#x20;

[Castile and León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castile_and_Le%C3%B3n) is the Spanish leader in [rural tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_tourism) linked to its environmental and architectural heritage[^cite_note-245] which it has kept since 2020.[^cite_note-246]&#x20;

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Spain)



The [Solucar Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solucar_Complex), with the [PS10 Solar Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PS10_Solar_Power_Plant) in the foreground and the [PS20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PS20_solar_power_plant) in the background

In 2010 Spain became the [solar power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_power_in_Spain) world leader when it overtook the United States with a massive power station plant called [La Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alvarado_I), near [Alvarado, Badajoz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badajoz).[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248] Spain is also Europe's main producer of wind energy.[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250] In 2010 its wind turbines generated 16.4% of all electrical energy produced in Spain.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252][^cite_note-Graber2005-253]&#x20;

In November 2010, wind power reached a historic peak, covering 53% of mainland electricity demand[^cite_note-254] and generating 14.2 GW of power, equivalent to that of fourteen [nuclear reactors](22151.md).[^cite_note-255] Other renewable energies used in Spain are [hydroelectric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_power_plant), [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gasification_of_biomass) and [marine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_energy).[^cite_note-256]&#x20;

Non-renewable energy sources used in Spain are [nuclear](207347.md) (eight operative reactors), [gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_power_plant), [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_power_plant), and [oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_power_plant). Fossil fuels together generated 58% of Spain's electricity in 2009, just below the OECD mean of 61%. Nuclear power generated another 19%, and wind and hydro about 12% each.[^cite_note-SverigeE-257]&#x20;

### Science and technology

Main article: [Science and technology in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Spain)



The [Gran Telescopio Canarias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gran_Telescopio_Canarias) at sunset

The [Consejo Superior de Investigaciones Científicas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consejo_Superior_de_Investigaciones_Cient%C3%ADficas) (CSIC) is the leading public agency dedicated to scientific research in the country. It ranked as the 5th top governmental scientific institution worldwide (and 32nd overall) in the 2018 SCImago Institutions Rankings.[^cite_note-258] Spain was ranked 29th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260]&#x20;

Higher education institutions perform about a 60% of the basic research in the country.[^cite_note-261] Likewise, the contribution of the private sector to R\&D expenditures is much lower than in other EU and OECD countries.[^cite_note-262]&#x20;

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Spain)

See also: [List of airports in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Spain)



A high-speed [AVE Class 103](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AVE_Class_103) train in [Zaragoza station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaragoza%E2%80%93Delicias_railway_station). Spain has [the longest high-speed rail network in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail_in_Spain).

[^cite_note-ADIF-263]

The Spanish road system is mainly centralised, with six highways connecting Madrid to the [Basque Country](20514897.md), [Catalonia](6822.md), [Valencia](20514168.md), West [Andalusia](2736.md), Extremadura and [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Spain\)). There are highways along the Atlantic ([Ferrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferrol) to [Vigo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vigo)), Cantabrian ([Oviedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oviedo) to [San Sebastián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Sebasti%C3%A1n)) and [Mediterranean](19006.md) ([Girona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girona) to [Cádiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A1diz)) coasts. Spain aims to put one million [electric cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_car) on the road by 2014 as part of the government's plan to save energy and boost [energy efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efficient_energy_use).[^cite_note-264] The former Minister of Industry [Miguel Sebastián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Sebasti%C3%A1n_Gasc%C3%B3n) said that "the electric vehicle is the future and the engine of an industrial revolution."[^cite_note-265]&#x20;

As of February 2025, the Spanish high-speed rail network is the longest HSR network in Europe with 3,973 km (2,469 mi)[^cite_note-ADIF-263] and the [second longest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high-speed_railway_lines), after China's. It is linking [Málaga](184763.md), [Seville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seville), Madrid, [Barcelona](4443.md), [Valencia](63861.md) and [Valladolid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valladolid), with the trains operated at commercial speeds up to 330 km/h (210 mph).[^cite_note-266] On average, the Spanish high-speed train is the fastest one in the world, followed by the Japanese [bullet train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinkansen) and the French [TGV](57212.md).[^cite_note-267] Regarding punctuality, it is second in the world (98.5% on-time arrival) after the Japanese Shinkansen (99%).[^cite_note-268]&#x20;

There are 49 public airports in Spain.[^cite_note-269] The busiest one is the [airport of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Airport) (Barajas), with 60 million passengers in 2023, being the [world's 15th busiest airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World%27s_busiest_airports_by_passenger_traffic), as well as the European Union's third busiest. The [airport of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcelona_Airport) (El Prat) is also important, with 50 million passengers in 2024, being the world's 30th-busiest airport.[^cite_note-270] Other main airports are located in [Mallorca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palma_de_Mallorca_Airport), [Málaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A1laga_Airport), [Las Palmas (Gran Canaria)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gran_Canaria_Airport), and [Alicante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alicante_Airport).[^cite_note-271]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Spain)

See also: [List of Spanish autonomous communities by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Spanish_autonomous_communities_by_population)



The population density by municipality in Spain, 2018



Population pyramid of Spain, 2021

[^cite_note-272]

In January 2026, Spain had a population of 49,570,725 people.[^cite_note-273] That same month, people born in Spain accounted for 80.7% of the total population and people born abroad accounted for 19.3% of the total population.[^cite_note-ine.es-274] Spain's [population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_density), at 98/km<sup>2</sup> (253.8/sq mi), is lower than that of most [Western European](33800.md) countries, and its distribution across the territory is highly uneven. With the notable exception of the region surrounding the capital, [Madrid](41188263.md), the most densely populated areas are located along the coast. The population of Spain has risen 2+1⁄2 times since 1900, when it stood at 18.6 million, principally due to the spectacular demographic boom in the 1960s and early 1970s.[^cite_note-275]&#x20;

In 2024, Spain's [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) (TFR) stood at 1.10 children per woman,[^cite_note-276] the second lowest in the European Union after [Malta](19137.md)[^cite_note-277] and among the lowest worldwide. This rate is well below the replacement level of 2.1 and contrasts sharply with the historical maximum of 5.11 children per woman recorded in 1865.[^cite_note-278] As a result, Spain has one of the oldest populations globally, with a [median age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_pyramid) of 46.8 years.[^cite_note-279]&#x20;

After the [birth rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_territories_by_fertility_rate) plunged in the 1980s and Spain's population growth rate dropped, the population again trended upward initially upon the return of many Spaniards who had emigrated to other European countries during the 1970s, and more recently, fuelled by large numbers of immigrants who make up more than 19% of the population today: Among non-EU immigrants, they are mainly from [Latin America](18524.md) (39%), [North Africa](21714.md) (16%), [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) (15%), and [Sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md) (4%).[^cite_note-280]&#x20;

In 2024, the number of foreign residents who acquired Spanish nationality increased by 5.1% to 252,476. The most frequent nationalities of origin were Morocco (42,910), Venezuela (30,403) and Colombia (26,224).[^cite_note-281] Spain has a number of descendants of populations from former colonies, especially Latin America and North Africa. Smaller numbers of immigrants from several [Sub-Saharan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-Saharan) countries have recently been settling in Spain. There are also sizeable numbers of Asian immigrants, most of whom are of Middle Eastern, [South Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian) and Chinese origin. The single largest group of immigrants are European; represented by large numbers of Romanians, Britons, [Germans](152735.md), French and others.[^cite_note-282]&#x20;

### Urbanisation

Main article: [List of metropolitan areas in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Spain)

In 2023, approximately 81.6% of Spain's population lived in [urban areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area), reflecting a continuing trend of [urbanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization) that has characterised Spain's demographic landscape in recent decades.[^cite_note-283] This concentration has contributed to the dominance of urban areas in [economic](27393.md), [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Spain), and [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Spain) spheres.&#x20;
