### Migration

Main article: [Immigration to Spain](10499603.md)



Distribution of the foreign population in Spain in 2005 by percentage



[Madrid](41188263.md), the 2nd biggest metropolitan area in the [European Union](9317.md), is home to over a million [Latin Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Americans).

[^cite_note-284]

As of January 2026, over ten million people in Spain were born abroad, accounting for 20.3% of the total population. Among them, over seven million (15.4%) were born outside Europe,[^cite_note-ine.es-274] and more than seven million (14.3%) held foreign nationality.[^cite_note-285] By [number of immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_by_immigrant_population), Spain ranks fourth in Europe and seventh worldwide.&#x20;

As of December 2024, the largest source of immigrants was Morocco (over one million), Colombia (856,616), Venezuela (599,769), Romania (532,456), and Ecuador (448,643).[^cite_note-poblacion-286] Most immigrants come from outside the EU, especially Latin America, with sizeable populations from the United Kingdom, Ukraine, China, Russia, Pakistan, Algeria, and Senegal.[^cite_note-poblacion-286]&#x20;

Historically a land of emigration, [immigration to Spain](10499603.md) has increased markedly since the 21st century, with immigrants rising from 1.6% of the population in 1998 to over 12% by 2009; in absolute terms, the number of immigrants grew from 500,000 in 1996 to 5.2 million in 2008. Spain was Europe's top recipient of migrants from 2002 to 2007, with 2.5 million arrivals.[^cite_note-287] In 2005, Spain had the second highest immigration rate in the European Union, after [Cyprus](5593.md), and the highest in absolute numbers,[^cite_note-288] with an amnesty programme increasing the legal immigrant population by 700,000.[^cite_note-289]&#x20;

The very high level of immigration is driven by Spain's cultural ties with Latin America, its geographical position, the porosity of its borders, the large size of its underground economy, and the strength of the agricultural and construction sectors, which demand more low-cost labour than can be offered by the national workforce. A large number of EU residents retire to Spain's Mediterranean coast;[^cite_note-290] in 2008, prior to the onset of the [economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008%E2%80%932014_Spanish_financial_crisis), the *Financial Times* reported that Spain was the most favoured destination for Western Europeans considering a move from their own country and seeking jobs elsewhere in the EU.[^cite_note-291]&#x20;

Until 2014, the number of immigrants decreased due to the [economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession), with more than half a million people leaving Spain in 2011 alone—the first net migration rate in decades.[^cite_note-emigracion-292] However, notwithstanding economic factors, as well as policies to encourage non-EU unemployed immigrants to voluntarily depart the country,[^cite_note-293] the rate of [emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration) remained relatively low.[^cite_note-294] By 2015, net immigration had increased, and Spain has again become a major immigration destination, backed by government policies to increase the labour force and promote integration.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296] The Sánchez government planned to legalize around 900,000 undocumented migrants by 2027.[^cite_note-297]&#x20;

In late July 2026, there was [a mass crossing of migrants from Morocco into Ceuta](83824713.md), with at least 141 fatalities among those attempting to reach Spain. By 3 August, Spanish government officials said about 70,000 people had voluntarily returned to Morocco.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299]&#x20;

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Spain)



A map of [Spain's langauages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Spain)

Spain is a multilingual state.[^cite_note-conversi-300] [Spanish](26825.md)—featured in the 1978 [Spanish Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Constitution) as *castellano* (['Castilian'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_given_to_the_Spanish_language))—has effectively been the official language of the entire country since 1931.[^cite_note-301] As allowed in the third article of the Constitution, the other 'Spanish languages' can also become official in their respective [autonomous communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_communities). The territoriality created by the form of co-officiality codified in the 1978 Constitution creates an asymmetry, in which Spanish speakers' rights apply to the entire territory whereas vis-à-vis the rest of co-official languages, their speakers' rights only apply in their territories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamallo2018465-302]&#x20;

Other territorialised languages include [Aragonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aragonese_language), [Aranese Occitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aranese), [Astur-Leonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astur-Leonese), [Basque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_language), Ceutan Arabic ([Darija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darija)), [Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language)/[Valencian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valencian_language), [Galician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician_language), [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language) and [Tamazight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarifit). [Romani Caló](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cal%C3%B3_language) and the sign languages are also used.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamallo2018462-303] The number of speakers varies widely and their legal recognition is uneven, with some of the most vulnerable languages lacking any sort of effective protection.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamallo2018463-304] Those enjoying recognition as an official language in some autonomous communities include Catalan/Valencian (in [Catalonia](6822.md) and the [Balearic Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balearic_Islands) officially named as Catalan and in the [Valencian Community](20514168.md) officially named as [Valencian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valencian_language)); Galician (in [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Spain\))); Basque (in the [Basque Country](20514897.md) and part of [Navarre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navarre)); and Aranese in Catalonia.&#x20;

In 2021, Spanish was the first language of 81.53% of the population, Catalan/Valencian was 10.50%, Galician was 3.77% and Basque was 1.42%. The most spoken foreign language was [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) with 2.17% of the population considering it as their first language, followed by [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) with 1.58%, [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language) with 1.44%, [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) with 0.94%, Portuguese with 0.54%, [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language) with 0.48%, [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language) with 0.44% and [Italian](14708.md) with 0.41%.[^cite_note-305] Such diversity of foreign languages is mainly due to international migration.[^cite_note-306]&#x20;

[Spanish](26825.md) is the world's [second-most spoken native language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_languages_by_number_of_native_speakers) and the world's most widely spoken [Romance language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_language).[^cite_note-cervantes.es-307]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Spain)



The [University of Salamanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Salamanca), one of the first European universities

State education in Spain is free and compulsory from the age of six to sixteen. The education system is regulated by the 2006 educational law, LOE (*Ley Orgánica de Educación*), or Fundamental Law for the Education.[^cite_note-LOE_juridicas-308] In 2014, the LOE was partially modified by the newer and controversial LOMCE law (*Ley Orgánica para la Mejora de la Calidad Educativa*), or Fundamental Law for the Improvement of the Education System, commonly called *Ley Wert* (Wert Law).[^cite_note-LOMCE_juridicas-309] Since 1970 to 2014, Spain has had seven different educational laws (LGE, LOECE, LODE, LOGSE, LOPEG, LOE and LOMCE).[^cite_note-310]&#x20;

The levels of education are preschool education, primary education,[^cite_note-311] secondary education[^cite_note-312] and post-16 education.[^cite_note-313] In regards to the professional development education or the vocational education, there are three levels besides the university degrees: the *Formación Profesional Básica* (basic vocational education); the *Ciclo Formativo de Grado Medio* or *CFGM* (medium level vocation education) which can be studied after studying the secondary education, and the *Ciclo Formativo de Grado Superior* or *CFGS* (higher level vocational education), which can be studied after studying the post-16 education level.[^cite_note-314]&#x20;

In 2021, the [Programme for International Student Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) (PISA) coordinated by the [OECD](33853117.md) ranked the overall knowledge and skills of Spanish 15-year-olds as significantly below the OECD average of 493 in reading literacy, mathematics, and science.[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316]&#x20;

### Health

Main articles: [Health care in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_Spain) and [Abortion in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Spain)

The health care system of Spain, the [Spanish National Health System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_National_Health_System), is considered one of the best in the world, ranked 7th position by the [World Health Organisation](33583.md) in 2000.[^cite_note-317] The health care is public, universal and free for any legal citizen of Spain.[^cite_note-318] Health spending is 9.4% of GDP, slightly above the average of 9.3% of the [OECD](33853117.md). The life expectancy in Spain has improved from 79.1 years in 2000 to 82.7 years in 2021.[^cite_note-319]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Spain)



The [Santiago de Compostela Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_de_Compostela_Cathedral)

[Roman Catholicism](606848.md), which has a long history in Spain, remains the dominant religion. Although it no longer has official status by law, in all public schools in Spain students have to choose either a religion or ethics class. Catholicism is the religion most commonly taught, although the teaching of Islam,[^cite_note-320] Judaism,[^cite_note-321] and evangelical Christianity[^cite_note-322] is also recognised in law. Recent polls and surveys suggest that around 30% of the Spanish population is irreligious.[^cite_note-CIS-323][^cite_note-WVS-324][^cite_note-GallupInternational-325]&#x20;

The Spanish constitution enshrines [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) in governance, as well as freedom of religion or belief for all, saying that no religion should have a "state character", while allowing for the state to "cooperate" with religious groups. [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) churches have about 1,200,000 members.[^cite_note-326] There are about 105,000 [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses). [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints) has approximately 46,000 adherents in 133 congregations.[^cite_note-327]&#x20;

A 2019 study made by the [Union of Islamic Communities of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Islamic_Communities_of_Spain) demonstrated that there were more than 2,100,000 inhabitants of Muslim background living in Spain, accounting for 4–5% of the population of Spain. The vast majority was composed of immigrants and descendants originating from the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb), especially [Morocco](19291.md), and other African countries. More than 879,000 (42%) of them had Spanish nationality.[^cite_note-328]&#x20;

[Judaism](15624.md) was practically non-existent in Spain from the 1492 expulsion until the 19th century, when Jews were again permitted to enter the country. Currently, there are around 62,000 Jews in Spain, or 0.14% of the total population.&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Spain)



[Guggenheim Museum Bilbao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guggenheim_Museum_Bilbao)

Spain is a [Western country](21208200.md) and one of the major [Latin countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance-speaking_Europe) of Europe, and has been noted for its international cultural influence.[^cite_note-r079-329] Spanish culture is marked by strong historic ties to the [Catholic Church](606848.md), which played a pivotal role in the country's formation and subsequent identity.[^cite_note-330] Spanish art, architecture, cuisine, and music have been shaped by successive waves of foreign invaders, as well as by the country's Mediterranean climate and geography. The centuries-long colonial era globalised Spanish language and culture, with Spain also [absorbing the cultural and commercial products of its diverse empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbian_exchange).&#x20;

[Bullfighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bullfighting) is widely regarded as one of Spain's most iconic cultural and artistic traditions, although it remains a subject of ongoing social and political debate.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332][^cite_note-l355-333] In 2013, it was officially recognized as part of the country's cultural heritage.[^cite_note-334]&#x20;

### World Heritage Sites

Main article: [World Heritage Sites in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites_in_Spain)

See also: [Castles in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castles_in_Spain) and [Cathedrals in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedrals_in_Spain)



[Alhambra](30543.md), [Granada](38264.md)

Spain has fifty [World Heritage Sites](44940.md). These include the landscape of [Monte Perdido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_Perdido) in the [Pyrenees](24707.md), which is shared with France, the Prehistoric Rock Art Sites of the [Côa Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B4a_Valley) and [Siega Verde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siega_Verde), which is shared with Portugal, the [Heritage of Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heritage_of_Mercury._Almad%C3%A9n_and_Idrija), shared with Slovenia and the [Ancient and Primeval Beech Forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_and_Primeval_Beech_Forests_of_the_Carpathians_and_Other_Regions_of_Europe), shared with other countries of Europe.[^cite_note-335] In addition, Spain also has fourteen items of [intangible cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_cultural_heritage), or "Human treasures".[^cite_note-336]&#x20;

### Philosophy

Main article: [Spanish philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_philosophy)

The construct pertaining a distinctive Spanish philosophical thought has been variously approached by academia, either by diachronically tracing its development throughout the centuries from the Roman conquest of Hispania on (with early representatives such as [Seneca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seneca_the_Young), [Trajan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trajan), [Lucan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucan), or [Martial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial)); by pinpointing its origins to the late 19th century (associated to the [Generation of 98](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_of_98)); or simply by outright denying its existence.[^cite_note-337] The crux around the existence of a Spanish philosophy pitted the likes of [Marcelino Menéndez y Pelayo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcelino_Men%C3%A9ndez_y_Pelayo) (chief architect of the myth around it)[^cite_note-338] against the legacy of anti-Spanish critique tracing back to figures like [Antonio Pérez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_P%C3%A9rez_\(statesman\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAntonovaMyagkovNikolaeva201967-339] Foreign imports such as [Krausism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krausism) proved to be extremely influential in Spain in the 19th and early 20th centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECaponigri1967169–170-340]&#x20;

### Literature

Main articles: [Spanish literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_literature), [Catalan literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_literature), [Galician literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician_literature), and [Basque literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_literature)

See also: [Latin American literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_literature), [Royal Spanish Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Spanish_Academy), and [Instituto Cervantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Cervantes)



Bronze statues of [Don Quixote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Quixote) and [Sancho Panza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sancho_Panza), at the [Plaza de España](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Espa%C3%B1a_\(Madrid\)) in [Madrid](41188263.md)

Some early examples of vernacular Romance-based literature include short snippets of [Mozarabic Romance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozarabic_language) (such as refrains) sprinkled in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) and [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) texts.[^cite_note-341] Other examples of early Iberian Romance include the *[Glosas Emilianenses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glosas_Emilianenses)* written in Latin, Basque and Romance.[^cite_note-342]&#x20;

Early Medieval literature in Christian Iberia was written in [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language), which remained as the standard literary language up until the mid-13th century, whereas Ibero-Romance vernaculars and Basque were spoken.[^cite_note-343] A decisive development ensued in the 13th century in [Toledo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toledo,_Spain), where Arabic scholarship was translated to the local vernacular, [Castilian](26825.md). In the scope of lyric poetry Castilian co-existed alongside [Galician-Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician-Portuguese) across the Crown of Castile up until the 16th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELabanyi201024-344] The Romance variety preferred in Eastern Iberia for lyrical poetry, [Occitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occitan_language), became increasingly [Catalanised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language) in the 14th and 15th centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELabanyi201021-345] Major literary works from the Middle Ages include the *[Cantar de Mio Cid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantar_de_Mio_Cid)*, *[Tirant lo Blanch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirant_lo_Blanch)*, *[The Book of Good Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Book_of_Good_Love)* and *[Coplas por la muerte de su padre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coplas_por_la_muerte_de_su_padre)*. Genres such as [Mester de Juglaría](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mester_de_Juglar%C3%ADa) and [Mester de Clerecía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mester_de_Clerec%C3%ADa) were cultivated.&#x20;

Promoted by the monarchs in the late Middle Ages and even codified in the late 15th century, Castilian (thought to be widespread known as 'Spanish' from the 16th century on) progressively became the language of the elites in the Iberian Peninsula, which ushered in a [Golden era of Castilian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Golden_Age) in the 16th and 17th centuries, also in the science domain, eclipsing Galician and Catalan.[^cite_note-346] Famous Early Modern works include *[La Celestina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Celestina)* and *[Lazarillo de Tormes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lazarillo_de_Tormes)*. The famous *[Don Quijote de La Mancha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Quixote)* by [Miguel de Cervantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_de_Cervantes) was written at this time. Other writers from the period are: [Francisco de Quevedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_de_Quevedo), [Lope de Vega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lope_de_Vega), [Calderón de la Barca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Calder%C3%B3n_de_la_Barca) or [Tirso de Molina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirso_de_Molina). During the [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) authors included [Benito Jerónimo Feijóo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Jer%C3%B3nimo_Feij%C3%B3o), [Gaspar Melchor de Jovellanos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaspar_Melchor_de_Jovellanos), and [Leandro Fernández de Moratín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leandro_Fern%C3%A1ndez_de_Morat%C3%ADn).&#x20;



[Federico García Lorca](100031.md) and other members of theatrical troupe "La Barraca"

Steps of [Spanish Romantic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism_in_Spanish_literature) (initially a rebellion against French classicism) have been traced back to the last quarter of the 18th century, even if the movement had its heyday between 1835 and 1850, waning thereafter.[^cite_note-347] In a broader definition encompassing the period from 1868 or 1874 to 1936, the so-called Silver Age of Spanish Culture ensued.[^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349]&#x20;

The waning of Romantic literature was followed by the development of [Spanish Realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Realist_literature), which offered depictions of contemporary life and society 'as they were', rather than romanticised or stylised presentations.[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] The major realist writer was [Benito Pérez Galdós](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_P%C3%A9rez_Gald%C3%B3s).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELabanyi201061-352] The second half of the 19th century also saw the resurgence of the literary use of local languages other than Spanish under cultural movements inspired by Romanticism such as the Catalan *[Renaixença](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaixen%C3%A7a)* or the Galician *[Rexurdimento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rexurdimento)*.[^cite_note-353] Rarely used before in a written medium, the true fostering of the literary use of the Basque language had to wait until the 1960s, even if some interest towards the language had developed in the late 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColuzzi2007103–104-354] 20th-century authors were classified in loose literary generations such as the [Generation of '98](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_of_1898), the [Generation of '27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_of_%2727), [Generation of '36](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_of_%2736) and the [Generation of '50](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_of_%2750).&#x20;

[Premio Planeta de Novela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premio_Planeta_de_Novela) and [Miguel de Cervantes Prize](3930514.md) are the two main awards in Spanish literature.&#x20;

### Art

Main article: [Spanish art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_art)



*[Les Demoiselles d'Avignon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Demoiselles_d%27Avignon)* (1907), [Pablo Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Picasso)

Artists from Spain have been highly influential in the development of various European and [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_America) [artistic movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_movement). Due to historical, geographical and generational diversity, Spanish art has known a great number of influences. The Mediterranean heritage with Greco-Roman and some Moorish influences in Spain, especially in [Andalusia](2736.md), is still evident today. European influences include Italy, Germany and France, especially during the Renaissance, [Spanish Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque) and [Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassicism) periods. There are many other autochthonous styles such as the [Pre-Romanesque art and architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Romanesque_art_and_architecture), [Herrerian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herrerian) architecture or the [Isabelline Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabelline_Gothic).[^cite_note-Arteespana-355][^cite_note-356][^cite_note-Durliat1966-357]&#x20;

During the Golden Age painters working in Spain included [El Greco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Greco), [José de Ribera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_Ribera), [Bartolomé Esteban Murillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartolom%C3%A9_Esteban_Murillo) and [Francisco Zurbarán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Zurbar%C3%A1n). Also, in the Baroque period, [Diego Velázquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez) created some of the most famous Spanish portraits, such as *[Las Meninas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Meninas)* and *[Las Hilanderas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Hilanderas_\(Vel%C3%A1zquez\))*.[^cite_note-358]&#x20;

[Francisco Goya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Goya) painted during a historical period that included the [Spanish Independence War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_War), the fights between liberals and absolutists, and the rise of contemporary nation-states.[^cite_note-359]&#x20;

[Joaquín Sorolla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_Sorolla) is a well-known modern Impressionist painter. There are many important Spanish painters belonging to the Modernist art movement, including [Pablo Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Picasso), [Salvador Dalí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Dal%C3%AD), [Juan Gris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Gris) and [Joan Miró](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_Mir%C3%B3).[^cite_note-360]&#x20;

### Sculpture

The Plateresque style extended from beginnings of the 16th century until the last third of the century and its stylistic influence pervaded the works of all great Spanish artists of the time. [Alonso Berruguete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alonso_Berruguete) ([Valladolid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valladolid) School) is called the "Prince of Spanish sculpture". His main works were the upper stalls of the choir of the [Cathedral of Toledo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_Toledo), the tomb of Cardinal Tavera in the same Cathedral, and the altarpiece of the Visitation in the church of Santa Úrsula in the same locality. Other notable sculptors were [Bartolomé Ordóñez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartolom%C3%A9_Ord%C3%B3%C3%B1ez), [Diego de Siloé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_de_Silo%C3%A9), [Juan de Juni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Juni) and [Damián Forment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dami%C3%A0_Forment).[^cite_note-ByneByne1917-361]&#x20;

There were two Schools: the [Seville School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sevillian_school_of_sculpture), to which [Juan Martínez Montañés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Mart%C3%ADnez_Monta%C3%B1%C3%A9s) belonged, whose most celebrated works are the Crucifix in the Cathedral of Seville, another in Vergara, and a Saint John; and the [Granada School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granadan_school_of_sculpture), to which [Alonso Cano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alonso_Cano) belonged, to whom an Immaculate Conception and a Virgin of Rosary are attributed.[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-newadvent.org-363]&#x20;

Other notable Andalusian Baroque sculptors were [Pedro de Mena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_de_Mena), [Pedro Roldán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Rold%C3%A1n) and his daughter [Luisa Roldán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luisa_Rold%C3%A1n), [Juan de Mesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Mesa) and [Pedro Duque Cornejo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Duque_Cornejo). In the 20th century the most important Spanish sculptors were [Julio González](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Gonz%C3%A1lez_\(sculptor\)), [Pablo Gargallo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Gargallo), [Eduardo Chillida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduardo_Chillida), and [Pablo Serrano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Serrano).&#x20;

### Architecture

Main article: [Spanish architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_architecture)



Basilica [Sagrada Família](59545.md) in [Barcelona](4443.md)

Earth and [gypsum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gypsum) are very common materials of the traditional [vernacular architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernacular_architecture) in Spain (particularly in the East of the country, where most of the deposits of gypsum are located).[^cite_note-364] Due to its historical and geographical diversity, Spanish architecture has drawn from a host of influences. Fine examples of [Islamicate architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_architecture), belonging to the [Western Islamic tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moorish_architecture), were built in the Middle Ages in places such as [Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba,_Spain), [Seville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seville), or [Granada](38264.md). Similarly to the Maghreb, [stucco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stucco) decoration in Al-Andalus became an architectural stylemark in the high Middle Ages.[^cite_note-365]&#x20;

Simultaneously, the Christian kingdoms also developed their own styles; developing a [pre-Romanesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Romanesque) style when isolated from contemporary mainstream European architectural influences during the earlier Middle Ages, they later integrated the [Romanesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanesque_architecture) and [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture) streams. The Gothic style resulted in numerous instances of Gothic architecture being built throughout the entire territory. The so-called [Mudéjar style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mud%C3%A9jar_art) came to designate works by Muslims, Christians and Jews in lands conquered from Muslims.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloom2020171-366]&#x20;

The arrival of [Modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism) produced much of the architecture of the 20th century. An influential style centred in [Barcelona](4443.md), known as [modernisme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernisme), produced a number of important architects, most notably [Antoni Gaudí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaud%C3%AD). The [International style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Style_\(architecture\)) was led by groups like [GATEPAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GATEPAC). Spain is currently experiencing a revolution in [contemporary architecture](3970948.md) and [Spanish architects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Spanish_architects) like [Rafael Moneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Moneo), [Santiago Calatrava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Calatrava), and [Ricardo Bofill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Bofill) (as well as others) have gained worldwide renown.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368]&#x20;

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Spain)

After the first projection of a cinematographer in Spain by 1896, cinema developed in the following years, with Barcelona becoming the largest production hub in the country (as well as a major European hub) on the eve of the World War I.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontes_Fernández2011602–603-369] The conflict offered the Spanish industry of [silent films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silent_film) an opportunity for further growth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontes_Fernández2011603-370] Local studios for [sound films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound_film) were created in 1932.[^cite_note-371] The government imposition of dubbing of foreign films in 1941 accustomed Spanish audiences to watching [dubbed films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubbing_\(filmmaking\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontes_Fernández2011609–610-372]&#x20;

Spanish cinema has achieved major international success including [Oscars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award) for films such as *[Pan's Labyrinth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan%27s_Labyrinth)* and *[Volver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volver)*.[^cite_note-373] Distinct exploitation genres that flourished in the second half of the 20th century include the *[Fantaterror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fantaterror)*, the [cine *quinqui*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cine_quinqui) and the so-called Cine de destape films.[^cite_note-374] As of 2021, the festivals of [San Sebastián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Sebasti%C3%A1n_International_Film_Festival) and [Málaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A1laga_Film_Festival) are ranked among the top cultural initiatives in the country.[^cite_note-375]&#x20;

### Music and dance

Main article: [Music of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Spain)



[Flamenco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flamenco) is an Andalusian artistic form that evolved from [Seguidilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seguidilla).

Spanish music is often considered abroad to be synonymous with [flamenco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flamenco), a West Andalusian musical genre, which is not widespread outside that region.[^cite_note-376] Various regional styles of [folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music) abound. Pop, rock, hip hop and heavy metal are also popular.&#x20;

In the field of classical music, Spain has produced a number of noted composers such as [Isaac Albéniz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Alb%C3%A9niz), [Manuel de Falla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_de_Falla) and [Enrique Granados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Granados) and singers and performers such as [Plácido Domingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pl%C3%A1cido_Domingo), [José Carreras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Carreras), [Montserrat Caballé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montserrat_Caball%C3%A9), [Alicia de Larrocha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alicia_de_Larrocha), [Alfredo Kraus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Kraus), [Pablo Casals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Casals), [Ricardo Viñes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Vi%C3%B1es), [José Iturbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Iturbi), [Pablo de Sarasate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_de_Sarasate), [Jordi Savall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordi_Savall) and [Teresa Berganza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresa_Berganza). In Spain there are over forty professional orchestras, including the [Orquestra Simfònica de Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcelona_Symphony_and_Catalonia_National_Orchestra), [Orquesta Nacional de España](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orquesta_Nacional_de_Espa%C3%B1a) and the [Orquesta Sinfónica de Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orquesta_Sinf%C3%B3nica_de_Madrid). Major [opera houses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opera_houses) include the [Teatro Real](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Real), the [Gran Teatre del Liceu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gran_Teatre_del_Liceu), [Teatro Arriaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Arriaga) and the [El Palau de les Arts Reina Sofía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Palau_de_les_Arts_Reina_Sof%C3%ADa).&#x20;

Thousands of music fans also travel to Spain each year for internationally recognised summer music festivals [Sónar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%B3nar) which features pop and techno acts, and [Benicàssim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festival_Internacional_de_Benic%C3%A0ssim) which tends to feature alternative rock and dance acts.[^cite_note-377] The [Vitoria-Gasteiz jazz festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitoria-Gasteiz_jazz_festival) is a notable jazz festival. One of the most popular traditional [musical instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_instrument), the guitar, originated in Spain.[^cite_note-378] Typical of the north are the traditional bag pipers or *[gaiteros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaita_\(bagpipe\))*, mainly in Asturias and Galicia.&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Spanish cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_cuisine)



[Paella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paella) mixta

Spanish cuisine consists of a great variety of dishes which stem from differences in geography, culture and climate. It is heavily influenced by seafood available from the waters that surround the country, and reflects the country's deep [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean) roots. Spain's extensive history with many cultural influences has led to a unique cuisine. In particular, three main divisions are easily identified:&#x20;

***Mediterranean*** Spain – coastal regions, from Catalonia to Andalusia – heavy use of seafood, such as *pescaíto frito* (fried fish); cold soups like *[gazpacho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazpacho)*; and many rice-based dishes like *[paella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paella)* from Valencia[^cite_note-paella-379] and *[arròs negre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arr%C3%B2s_negre)* (black rice) from Catalonia.[^cite_note-380]&#x20;

***Inner*** Spain – Castile – hot, thick soups such as the bread and garlic-based *Castilian soup*, along with substantial stews such as *[cocido madrileño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocido_madrile%C3%B1o)*. Food is traditionally preserved by salting, such as [Spanish ham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_ham), or immersed in [olive oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil), such as [Manchego cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchego_cheese).&#x20;

***Atlantic*** Spain – the Northern coast, including [Asturian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asturian_cuisine), [Basque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_cuisine), [Cantabrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantabrian_cuisine) and [Galician cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician_cuisine) – vegetable and fish-based stews like *[caldo gallego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caldo_gallego)* and *[marmitako](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmitako)*. Also, the lightly cured *[lacón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lac%C3%B3n_Gallego)* ham. The best known cuisine of the northern countries often rely on ocean seafood, as in the Basque-style [cod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cod), [albacore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albacore) or [anchovy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchovy) or the Galician octopus-based *[polbo á feira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polbo_%C3%A1_feira)* and shellfish dishes.&#x20;

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Spain)



[Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_women%27s_national_football_team) or *La Roja* celebrating their [2023 FIFA Women's World Cup](45271353.md) victory

While varieties of [football](957951.md) have been played in Spain as far back as Roman times, sport in Spain has been dominated by football since the early 20th century. [Real Madrid CF](26413.md) and [FC Barcelona](68187.md) are two of the most successful football clubs in the world. [The country's national men's football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_men%27s_national_football_team) won the [UEFA European Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_European_Championship) in [1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_European_Nations%27_Cup), [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2008), [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2012) and [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2024) and the [FIFA World Cup](11370.md) in [2010](168079.md) and [2026](36581929.md), and is the first team ever to win three back-to-back major international tournaments.[^cite_note-381] [Spain's women's national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_women%27s_national_football_team) were champions of the [2023 FIFA Women's World Cup](45271353.md), becoming one of only five nations to win the [Women's World Cup](244862.md). [Barcelona Femení](24763114.md) has won a record number of domestic trophies.[^cite_note-382]&#x20;

[Basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_in_Spain), [tennis](29067598.md)—where players like [Rafa Nadal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafa_Nadal) and [Carlos Alcaraz](63121147.md) stand out—cycling, [handball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Team_handball), [futsal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_national_futsal_team), [motorcycling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorcycling) and, lately, [Formula One](10854.md) also can boast of Spanish champions. Today, Spain is a major world sports powerhouse, especially since the [1992 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Summer_Olympics) and [Paralympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Summer_Paralympics) that were hosted in [Barcelona](4443.md), which stimulated a great deal of interest in sports in the country. The tourism industry has led to an improvement in sports infrastructure, especially for [water sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_sports), [golf](19568112.md) and [skiing](28478.md). In their respective regions, the traditional games of [Basque pelota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_pelota) and [Valencian pilota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valencian_pilota) are both popular.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384]&#x20;

### Public holidays and festivals

Main articles: [National Day of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_of_Spain), [Public holidays in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Spain), [Fiestas of International Tourist Interest of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiestas_of_International_Tourist_Interest_of_Spain), and [Fiestas of National Tourist Interest of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiestas_of_National_Tourist_Interest_of_Spain)



[Semana Santa in La Laguna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Week_in_San_Crist%C3%B3bal_de_La_Laguna), [Tenerife](269223.md)

Public holidays celebrated in Spain include a mix of religious ([Roman Catholic](606848.md)), national and local observances. Each municipality is allowed to declare a maximum of fourteen public holidays per year; up to nine of these are chosen by the national government and at least two are chosen locally.[^cite_note-385] [Spain's National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_of_Spain) (*Fiesta Nacional de España*) is celebrated on 12 October.[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387]&#x20;

There are many festivals and festivities in Spain. One of the most famous is [San Fermín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festival_of_San_Ferm%C3%ADn), in [Pamplona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamplona). Its most famous event is the *encierro*, or the [running of the bulls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Running_of_the_bulls). It has become one of the most internationally renowned fiestas in Spain, with over a million people attending every year.[^cite_note-388]&#x20;

Other festivals include [La Tomatina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Tomatina) tomato festival in [Buñol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bu%C3%B1ol), [Valencia](20514168.md), the carnivals in the [Canary Islands](5717.md), the [Falles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falles) in [Valencia](63861.md) or the [Holy Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Week_in_Spain) in Andalusia and [Castile and León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castile_and_Le%C3%B3n).&#x20;

### Media

In Spain, TV is the main source of news and satellite and cable are popular digital platforms. Home-produced dramas, reality shows and long-running soap operas are staple fare on primetime TV. Regional TV networks are operated by their respective governments and there are many local stations. The biggest TV provider is Movistar+, owned by telecom firm Telefónica.[^cite_note-389]&#x20;

On 11 March 2026, Spanish Prime Minister [Pedro Sanchez](43297512.md) announced that he will launch HODIO, a tool which will allow the government to systematically track the presence, amplification and impact of hate speech online, to measure hate speech on digital platforms as part of a broader strategy to increase oversight of social media companies.[^cite_note-390]&#x20;

[^cite_note-47]: &#x20;The poets [Martial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial), [Quintilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quintilian) and [Lucan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucan) were also born in Hispania.

[^cite_note-130]: &#x20;Those nationwide institutions are the Crown, the Cortes Generales, the Government, the Judiciary, and the Constitutional Tribunal.

[^cite_note-134]: &#x20;Most [Spanish monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Spanish_monarchs) have been kings. However, a [queen regnant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_regnant) – while uncommon – is possible due to Spain's adherence to [male-preference primogeniture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Male-preference_primogeniture). [Leonor, Princess of Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonor,_Princess_of_Asturias), would be Spain's first queen regnant since [Isabella II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabella_II), who reigned from 1833 to 1868, should she someday succeed her father [Felipe VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_VI) as expected.

[^cite_note-146]: &#x20;Former king [Juan Carlos I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Carlos_I)'s intervention and foiling of the [1981 Spanish coup attempt](546272.md) is but one example of the Crown exercising its influence as the moderating branch to defend democracy and uphold the rule of law.<sup>[\[133\]](#cite_note-145)</sup>

[^cite_note-163]: &#x20;The Spanish state honours system comprises the [Order of Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Charles_III), the [Order of Isabella the Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Isabella_the_Catholic), the [Order of Civil Merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Civil_Merit), the [Civil Order of Alfonso X, the Wise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Order_of_Alfonso_X,_the_Wise), the [Order of Saint Raymond of Peñafort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Raymond_of_Pe%C3%B1afort), and the [Order of Constitutional Merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Constitutional_Merit), among other orders, decorations and medals. The prime minister is [ex officio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ex_officio) chancellor of the Order of Charles III. On the other hand, the ministers of [foreign affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Spain\)), [education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(Spain\)), and [justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(Spain\)) are the corresponding chancellors for the orders of Isabella the Catholic and of Civil Merit, the Civil Order of Alfonso X, the Wise, and the orders of Saint Raymond of Peñafort and of Constitutional Merit, respectively.

[^cite_note-170]: &#x20;These being the [Real Academia de Bellas Artes de San Fernando](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Academia_de_Bellas_Artes_de_San_Fernando), the [Real Academia de Ciencias Morales y Políticas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Academia_de_Ciencias_Morales_y_Pol%C3%ADticas), the [Royal Academy of Engineering of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Academy_of_Engineering_of_Spain), the [Real Academia de la Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Academia_de_la_Historia), the [Royal Academy of Jurisprudence and Legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Academy_of_Jurisprudence_and_Legislation), the [Royal Academy of Pharmacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Academy_of_Pharmacy), the [Royal Spanish Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Spanish_Academy), and the [Spanish Royal Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Royal_Academy_of_Sciences)
